[Pre-training](#pre-training) - The Foundation Phase\n5: 2. [Fine-tuning](#fine-tuning) - Adapting to Specific Tasks\n6: 3. [Comparison: Pre-training vs Fine-tuning](#comparison-pretraining-vs-finetuning)\n7: 4. [Inference Strategies](#inference-strategies)\n8:    - Quantization\n9:    - Pruning\n10:    - Knowledge Distillation\n11:    - Attention Optimization\n12: 5. [Best Practices and Guidelines](#best-practices-and-guidelines)\n13: 6. [Use Cases and Recommendations](#use-cases-and-recommendations)\n14: \n15: ---\n16: \n17: ## Pre-training: The Foundation Phase\n18: \n19: ### What is Pre-training?\n20: \n21: Pre-training is the process of training a transformer model on large-scale, diverse datasets to learn fundamental language representations and patterns before being adapted for specific downstream tasks. This two-phase paradigm has become dominant in modern deep learning because it's more efficient, effective, and requires less labeled data [Medium, 2026].\n22: \n23: ### Key Characteristics:\n24: \n25: **Data Requirements:**\n26: - Uses massive corpora (hundreds of billions to trillions of tokens)\n27: - Typically web-scraped text from Common Crawl, Wikipedia, books, etc.\n28: - Unsupervised or self-supervised learning objectives (e.g., masked language modeling for BERT, next-token prediction for GPT)\n29: \n30: **Training Objectives:**\n31: - **BERT-style**: Masked Language Modeling (MLM) + Next Sentence Prediction (NSP)\n32: - **GPT-style**: Causal language modeling (predict next token)\n33: - Other objectives: contrastive learning, autoregressive reasoning\n34: \n35: **Compute Requirements:**\n36: - Extremely high GPU/TPU resources needed\n37: - Training can take weeks to months on large-scale clusters\n38: - Example: GPT-3 trained on ~45TB of text using 10k+ GPUs for several months\n39: \n40: ### Pre-training Phases:\n41: \n42: ```\n43: Phase 1: Raw Data Collection & Cleaning\n44:         ↓\n45: Phase 2: Tokenization & Processing (e.g., BPE, WordPiece)\n46:         ↓\n47: Phase 3: Model Initialization (random weights)\n48:         ↓\n49: Phase 4: Large-scale Training on diverse corpus\n50:         ↓\n51: Phase 5: Evaluation on benchmark datasets\n52:         ↓\n53: Output: Pre-trained model with learned general representations\n54: ```\n55: \n56: ### Benefits of Pre-training:\n57: \n58: 1. **Learn General Language Understanding**: Captures grammar, semantics, world knowledge from vast text exposure\n59: 2. **Transfer Learning Foundation**: Provides robust starting point for many downstream tasks\n60: 3. **Data Efficiency**: Downstream tasks need far less labeled data (often 10-100x less than training from scratch)\n61: 4. **Zero-shot/Few-shot Capabilities**: Can perform on tasks without explicit fine-tuning\n62: \n63: ---\n64: \n65: ## Fine-tuning: Adapting to Specific Tasks\n66: \n67: ### What is Fine-tuning?\n68: \n69: Fine-tuning takes a pre-trained model and further trains it on a smaller, task-specific dataset with specific labels or instructions. This leverages the general capabilities learned during pre-training while adapting them to domain knowledge [Hugging Face].\n70: \n71: ### Types of Fine-tuning:\n72: \n73: #### 1. Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT)\n74: - Uses labeled input-output pairs\n75: - Example: Classification, question answering, summarization datasets\n76: - Model learns to map inputs to correct outputs for the target task\n77: \n78: #### 2. Instruction Fine-Tuning\n79: - Uses instruction-following prompts and responses\n80: - Enables chatbot behavior and complex multi-step tasks\n81: - Often used for LLM alignment (e.g., Alpaca, Vicuna)\n82: \n83: #### 3. Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT)\n84: - **LoRA**: Low-rank adaptation - freezes most parameters, trains small adapter layers\n85: - **Adapter Layers**: Insert trainable modules between transformer blocks\n86: - **Prefix Tuning**: Adds trainable prefix tokens to model inputs\n87: - Reduces compute/memory requirements by 10-50x\n88: \n89: ### Fine-tuning Phases:\n90: \n91: ```\n92: Input: Pre-trained model + Task-specific dataset (smaller)\n93:         ↓\n94: Model Loading with pre-trained weights\n95:         ↓\n96: Task-Specific Training Loop\n97:    - Lower learning rate than pre-training\n98:    - Fewer epochs typically needed\n99:         ↓\n100: Evaluation on task-specific benchmarks\n101: Output: Task-specialized model with adapted representations\n102: ```\n103: \n104: ### Fine-tuning Characteristics:\n105: \n106: **Data Requirements:**\n107: - Often 10,000 to 1 million labeled examples (vs billions for pre-training)\n108: - High-quality, curated datasets preferred over large noisy ones\n109: - Domain-specific data improves performance significantly\n110: \n111: **Compute Requirements:**\n112: - Significantly less than pre-training (hours to days vs weeks/months)\n113: - Can run on consumer-grade GPUs (e.g., 1-4× A100/H100 or even RTX 3090/4090 for smaller models)\n114: - PEFT methods allow fine-tuning on single GPU\n115: \n116: **Key Differences from Pre-training:**\n117: \n118: | Aspect | Pre-training | Fine-tuning |\n119: |--------|--------------|-------------|\n120: | Learning Rate | High (1e-4 to 5e-5) | Low (2e-5 to 5e-6) |\n121: | Batch Size | Large (thousands) | Smaller (hundreds to thousands) |\n122: | Epochs | Many (dozens/hundreds) | Fewer (1-10 typically) |\n123: | Data Type | Unsupervised/semi-supervised | Supervised (labeled pairs) |\n124: | Goal | Learn general representations | Adapt to specific task |\n125: \n126: ---\n127: \n128: ## Comparison: Pre-training vs Fine-tuning\n129: \n130: ### Side-by-Side Analysis:\n131: \n132: #### Training Paradigm:\n133: ```\n134: ┌─────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────┐\n135: │   PRE-TRAINING      │   FINE-TUNING                               │\n136: ├─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┤\n137: │ Start: Random       │ Start: Pre-trained weights                  │\n138: │ Weights             │ (not random initialization)                 │\n139: ├─────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────┤\n140: │ Data Volume         │   100B+ tokens                              │\n141: │                    │   Fine-tuning: 1M-100M tokens               │\n142: ├─────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────┤\n143: │ Learning Objective  │   General language/patterns                 │\n144: │                    │   Task-specific mappings                    │\n145: ├─────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────┤\n146: │ Model Output        │   Foundation model (e.g., GPT, BERT)       │\n147: │                    │   Task-specialized model (e.g., sentiment classifier) │\n148: └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘\n149: ```\n150: \n151: #### When to Use Each:\n152: \n153: **Pre-training is appropriate when:**\n154: - Building a new foundation model from scratch\n155: - You have access to massive datasets and compute resources\n156: - Creating models for diverse downstream applications\n157: - Research on novel architectures or objectives\n158: \n159: **Fine-tuning is appropriate when:**\n160: - Adapting existing pre-trained models to specific tasks/domains\n161: - Limited labeled data available (fine-tuning needs less)\n162: - Budget/compute constraints exist (fine-tuning much cheaper)\n163: - Rapid iteration needed (fine-tuning faster)\n164: \n165: #### The Pre-training/Fine-tuning Paradigm:\n166: \n167: > \"The pre-training/fine-tuning paradigm has become dominant because it's more efficient, more effective, and requires less labeled data.\" - Medium, Jan 2026 [1]\n168: \n169: This two-phase approach enables transfer learning at scale. Research shows that early stopping in pre-training is not detrimental to downstream fine-tuning performance when budget is constrained [arXiv, Mar 2025].\n170: \n171: ### Practical Example: Sentiment Analysis Pipeline\n172: \n173: ```\n174: ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐\n175: │  PRE-TRAINING PHASE                                             │\n176: │  ├─ Dataset: Wikipedia + Common Crawl (1TB+ text)               │\n177: │  ├─ Objective: Next-token prediction                             │\n178: │  ├─ Duration: ~3 months on 10,000 GPUs                          │\n179: │  └─ Output: Pre-trained GPT-2 style model                       │\n180: ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤\n181: │  SUPERVISED FINE-TUNING PHASE                                   │\n182: │  ├─ Dataset: IMDB reviews (50K labeled examples)                │\n183: │  ├─ Learning Rate: 2e-5                                         │\n184: │  ├─ Epochs: 3                                                   │\n185: │  └─ Duration: ~1 day on 4× A100 GPUs                            │\n186: └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘\n187: \n188: Result: Model achieves 94% accuracy (vs 85% from random init)\n189: ```\n190: \n191: ---\n192: \n193: ## Inference Strategies\n194: \n195: Once a model is trained or fine-tuned, inference optimization becomes critical for deployment. This section covers major strategies to make transformer models faster and more efficient at serving time.\n196: \n197: ### Quantization\n198: \n199: #### What is Quantization?\n200: \n201: Quantization reduces the numerical precision of model weights and activations from 32-bit floating point (FP32) to lower precisions like:\n202: - **INT8**: 8-bit integers\n203: - **INT4**: 4-bit integers\n204: - **FP16/BF16**: Half precision\n205: \n206: #### Types of Quantization:\n207: \n208: **Post-training Quantization:**\n209: - Converts model weights after training without retraining\n210: - Requires calibration data to determine optimal quantization ranges\n211: - Simpler but may lose some accuracy\n212: \n213: **Quantization-Aware Training (QAT):**\n214: - Simulates low-precision operations during training\n215: - More accurate than post-training quantization\n216: - Computationally more expensive during training\n217: \n218: #### Quantization Levels Comparison:\n219: \n220: | Precision | Memory Usage | Speedup | Accuracy Loss | Use Case |\n221: |-----------|--------------|---------|---------------|----------|\n222: | FP32      | 100%         | 1x      | -             | Baseline |\n223: | BF16/FP16 | 50%          | ~2x     | <1%           | GPU inference |\n224: | INT8      | 25%          | ~4x     | 1-3%          | CPU/TensorRT |\n225: | INT4      | 12.5%        | ~8x     | 2-5%          | Edge devices |\n226: \n227: #### Best Practices:\n228: \n229: ```python\n230: # Example: Hugging Face quantization for inference\n231: from transformers import AutoModelForCausalLM, AutoTokenizer\n232: import torch\n233: \n234: model = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained(\n235:     \"meta-llama/Llama-2-7b\", \n236:     load_in_4bit=True  # INT4 quantization\n237: )\n238: \n239: # Or use bitsandbytes for more control\n240: from bitsandbytes import optimize_module\n241: model = optimize_module(model, method=\"nf4\")\n242: ```\n243: \n244: ### Pruning\n245: \n246: #### What is Pruning?\n247: \n248: Pruning removes unnecessary weights or neurons from a trained model to reduce its size and computational requirements. This can be done:\n249: - **Structured**: Removing entire filters/channels (preserves tensor structure)\n250: - **Unstructured**: Randomly removing individual weights (requires sparsity-aware hardware)\n251: \n252: #### Types of Pruning:\n253: \n254: **Weight Pruning:**\n255: - Removes least important connections based on magnitude or importance scores\n256: - Methods: Magnitude-based, sensitivity analysis, gradient-based\n257: \n258: **Channel/Filter Pruning:**\n259: - Removes entire convolutional filters or attention heads\n260: - More aggressive but preserves model structure better\n261: \n262: **Progressive Pruning:**\n263: - Iteratively prunes and retrains to maintain accuracy\n264: - Starts with small pruning ratios (1%) and increases gradually\n265: \n266: #### Pruning Strategies:\n267: \n268: ```\n269: ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐\n270: │  PRUNING PIPELINE                                               │\n271: │                                                                 │\n272: │  1. Train full model                                            │\n273: │     ↓                                                            │\n274: │  2. Compute importance scores (weights, channels)               │\n275: │     ↓                                                            │\n276: │  3. Identify least important components                         │\n277: │     ↓                                                            │\n278: │  4. Remove/zero out selected components                        │\n279: │     ↓                                                            │\n280: │  5. Optional: Fine-tune to recover accuracy                    │\n281: └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘\n282: ```\n283: \n284: #### Pruning vs Quantization Trade-offs:\n285: \n286: | Aspect | Pruning | Quantization | Combined Approach |\n287: |--------|---------|--------------|-------------------|\n288: | Memory Reduction | High (up to 90%) | Moderate (75%) | Very High |\n289: | Speedup | Variable | High (2-4x) | Highest |\n290: | Accuracy Impact | Can be significant | Low-moderate | Managed with QAT |\n291: | Hardware Support | Limited (sparse ops) | Widely supported | Best compatibility |\n292: \n293: ### Knowledge Distillation\n294: \n295: #### What is Knowledge Distillation?\n296: \n297: Knowledge distillation transfers knowledge from a large \"teacher\" model to a smaller \"student\" model. The student learns not just from hard labels but also from the teacher's softened output distributions.\n298: \n299: ```\n300: ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐\n301: │  KNOWLEDGE DISTILLATION                                          │\n302: │                                                                 │\n303: │         TEACHER MODEL (Large, Slow)                             │\n304: │              ↓ Softmax(T=10)                                    │\n305: │              ↓                                                  │\n306: │    ┌───────────────────────┐                                   │\n307: │    │   STUDENT MODEL       │                                   │\n308: │    │   (Small, Fast)       │  Hard labels + Soft targets        │\n309: │    └───────────────────────┘                                   │\n310: └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘\n311: ```\n312: \n313: #### Distillation Techniques:\n314: \n315: 1. **Logit Temperature Scaling**: Uses softened probability distributions during training\n316: 2. **Feature-based Distillation**: Matches hidden layer activations between teacher and student\n317: 3. **Attention Distillation**: Transfers attention patterns from teacher to student\n318: \n319: #### Use Cases:\n320: - Deploying models on edge devices with limited memory\n321: - Reducing inference latency while maintaining acceptable accuracy\n322: - Creating specialized domain models from foundation models\n323: \n324: ### Attention Optimization\n325: \n326: #### Memory-Efficient Attention Algorithms\n327: \n328: Standard self-attention has O(n²) complexity in sequence length, which becomes prohibitive for long contexts. Several optimizations address this:\n329: \n330: **FlashAttention:**\n331: - Reorganizes computation to improve memory access patterns\n332: - Reduces HBM (high-bandwidth memory) usage by 3-5x\n333: - Maintains numerical accuracy while enabling longer context windows\n334: \n335: **Sparse Attention:**\n336: - Only attends to important positions rather than all positions\n337: - Methods: Sliding window, global-local attention, ring attention\n338: - Significantly reduces compute for long sequences\n339: \n340: #### QKV Cache Optimization:\n341: \n342: ```python\n343: # Standard attention recomputes QKV each layer\n344: # Optimized approach caches QKV matrices\n345: \n346: class OptimizedTransformerBlock(torch.nn.Module):\n347:     def forward(self, x):\n348:         # Compute once per sequence\n349:         q, k, v = self.qkv(x)  \n350:         cache_key = (k.transpose(-2,-1), v)  # Cache for future layers\n351:         \n352:         # Decode from cache (no recomputation needed)\n353:         if hasattr(self, 'cached_kv'):\n354:             output = scaled_dot_product_attention(q, self.cached_kv[0], self.cached_kv[1])\n355:         else:\n356:             output = scaled_dot_product_attention(q, k, v)\n357:         \n358:         return output\n359: ```\n360: \n361: #### Attention Variants for Efficiency:\n362: \n363: | Method | Description | Speedup | Use Case |\n364: |--------|-------------|---------|----------|\n365: | FlashAttention | Memory-efficient blocks | 3-5x | Long context |\n366: | Sparse Attention | Selective attention heads | 2-4x | Very long sequences |\n367: | Mixture of Experts (MoE) | Activates subset of experts | 1.5-2x | Large models |\n368: \n369: ---\n370: \n371: ## Best Practices and Guidelines\n372: \n373: ### Pre-training Best Practices:\n374: \n375: #### Data Strategy:\n376: ```python\n377: # Recommended data preprocessing pipeline\n378: def prepare_pretraining_data(raw_text):\n379:     \"\"\"\n380:     Key steps for pre-training data preparation:\n381:     1. Text cleaning (remove HTML, special chars)\n382:     2. Tokenization (BPE recommended for subword learning)\n383:     3. Sequence masking strategy\n384:     4. Vocabulary building (handle rare tokens appropriately)\n385:     \"\"\"\n386: ```\n387: \n388: #### Training Configuration Recommendations:\n389: \n390: | Hyperparameter | Recommended Range | Notes |\n391: |----------------|-------------------|-------|\n392: | Learning Rate | 1e-4 to 5e-5 | Warmup first 10% of steps |\n393: | Batch Size | 2K-8K tokens/batch | Scale with GPU count |\n394: | Sequence Length | 1k-8k tokens | Depends on task and resources |\n395: | Max Gradient Norm | 1.0 | Prevents exploding gradients |\n396: \n397: #### Loss Function Selection:\n398: \n399: ```python\n400: # Common objectives for pre-training:\n401: # - Causal LM (GPT-style): CrossEntropyLoss(next_token_logits, target_ids)\n402: # - Masked LM (BERT-style): CrossEntropyLoss(masked_positions, predicted_tokens)\n403: # - Contrastive Learning: InfoNCE loss for embedding alignment\n404: ```\n405: \n406: ### Fine-tuning Best Practices:\n407: \n408: #### Hyperparameter Tuning:\n409: \n410: | Parameter | Fine-tuning Range | Pre-training Range |\n411: |-----------|-------------------|--------------------|\n412: | Learning Rate | 1e-5 to 2e-5 | 3e-4 to 1e-4 |\n413: | Batch Size | 8-256 | 2048+ |\n414: | Epochs | 1-10 | Dozens-Hundreds |\n415: \n416: #### PEFT Configuration:\n417: \n418: ```python\n419: from peft import LoraConfig, get_peft_model\n420: \n421: # LoRA configuration for fine-tuning\n422: lora_config = LoraConfig(\n423:     r=16,                      # Rank of adapters (8-32 typical)\n424:     lora_alpha=32,             # Scale factor\n425:     target_modules=[\"q_proj\", \"v_proj\"],  # Key modules to adapt\n426:     task_type=\"CAUSAL_LM\"      # or SEQ_CLS for classification\n427: )\n428: \n429: model = get_peft_model(base_model, lora_config)\n430: ```\n431: \n432: #### Early Stopping and Monitoring:\n433: \n434: - Monitor validation loss every N steps (not just at epoch boundaries)\n435: - Use learning rate warmup followed by linear decay\n436: - Implement gradient accumulation for effective larger batches\n437: \n438: ### Inference Best Practices:\n439: \n440: #### Model Loading Strategies:\n441: \n442: ```python\n443: # Efficient model loading with quantization\n444: from transformers import AutoModelForCausalLM, AutoTokenizer\n445: import torch\n446: \n447: def load_model_for_inference(model_name, device=\"cuda\", use_quantization=False):\n448:     \"\"\"Load model optimized for inference\"\"\"\n449:     \n450:     if use_quantization and device == \"cpu\":\n451:         # Use 8-bit quantization for CPU inference\n452:         from bitsandbytes import AutoModelForCausalLM as BnbAutoModel\n453:         \n454:         model = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained(\n455:             model_name, \n456:             load_in_8bit=True,\n457:             device_map=\"auto\"\n458:         )\n459:     else:\n460:         # Use BF16 for GPU (better precision than FP32)\n461:         model = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained(\n462:             model_name,\n463:             torch_dtype=torch.bfloat16 if device == \"cuda\" else torch.float32\n464:         )\n465:     \n466:     return model.eval()\n467: ```\n468: \n469: #### Batch Processing for Inference:\n470: \n471: ```python\n472: def batch_inference(model, tokenizer, prompts):\n473:     \"\"\"\n474:     Process multiple inputs in a single forward pass for efficiency.\n475:     Key optimization: Combine requests with similar sequence lengths.\n476:     \"\"\"\n477:     # 1. Pad sequences to same length\n478:     # 2. Create attention masks for variable-length inputs\n479:     # 3. Single batched inference call\n480:     # 4. Unpack and process individual outputs\n481: ```\n482: \n483: #### Caching Strategies:\n484: \n485: - **KV Cache**: Store key-value matrices across layers for multi-turn conversations\n486: - **Prefill Optimization**: Run prefill (prompt processing) and decode separately\n487: - **Continuous Batching**: Keep GPU busy by overlapping requests\n488: \n489: ---\n490: \n491: ## Use Cases and Recommendations\n492: \n493: ### When to Pre-train vs Fine-tune: Decision Matrix\n494: \n495: ```\n496: ┌─────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────┐\n497: │   Scenario              │   Recommended Approach        │\n498: ├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤\n499: │ New domain (e.g., legal) │ Pre-train on domain corpus    │\n500: │                         │ + Fine-tune on tasks          │\n501: ├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤\n502: │ Standard NLP task       │ Direct fine-tuning           │\n503: │ (classification, etc.)   │                              │\n504: ├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤\n505: │ Very small dataset      │ Fine-tune with PEFT          │\n506: │ (<10K examples)         │ or use prompt engineering    │\n507: ├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤\n508: │ Real-time inference     │ Use quantized pre-trained    │\n509: │ model (latency <50ms)   │                              │\n510: ├─────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤\n511: │ Research/experimentation │ Start with fine-tuning       │\n512: │                         │ for rapid iteration          │\n513: └─────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────┘\n514: ```\n515: \n516: ### Cost-Benefit Analysis:\n517: \n518: | Approach | Compute Cost | Time to Deploy | Accuracy | Use Case |\n519: |----------|--------------|----------------|----------|----------|\n520: | Train from scratch | Very High | Weeks-Months | Baseline | Research, custom architecture |\n521: | Pre-train + Fine-tune | Medium-High | Days-Weeks | Best | Production systems |\n522: | Direct fine-tuning (PEFT) | Low | Hours-Days | Good | Rapid prototyping |\n523: | Prompt Engineering | Very Low | Minutes | Variable | Simple tasks, experimentation |\n524: \n525: ### Recommended Pipeline for Most Applications:\n526: \n527: ```\n528: ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐\n529: │  PRODUCTION RECOMMENDATION PIPELINE                                  │\n530: │                                                                      │\n531: │  Phase 1: Foundation Model Selection                                  │\n532: │         ├─ Choose from pre-trained models (HuggingFace, etc.)       │\n533: │         └─ Consider model size vs. your compute budget               │\n534: │                                                                      │\n535: │  Phase 2: Domain Adaptation                                           │\n536: │         ├─ If domain-specific: Fine-tune on domain corpus            │\n537: │         ├─ If task-specific: Direct fine-tuning                       │\n538: │         └─ Use PEFT if GPU resources limited                         │\n539: │                                                                      │\n540: │  Phase 3: Inference Optimization                                      │\n541: │         ├─ Apply quantization (INT8/INT4)                            │\n542: │         ├─ Enable KV caching                                          │\n543: │         └─ Use batch processing for throughput                        │\n544: └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘\n545: ```\n546: \n547: ### Specific Recommendations by Model Size:\n548: \n549: #### Small Models (<1B parameters):\n550: - Can be fine-tuned on consumer GPUs (RTX 3090/4090)\n551: - Use full precision or INT8 quantization\n552: - Suitable for specialized tasks with limited data\n553: \n554: #### Medium Models (1-7B parameters):\n555: - Require enterprise GPU(s) or cloud instances\n556: - INT4 quantization recommended for deployment\n557: - Good balance of capability and efficiency\n558: \n559: #### Large Models (>70B parameters):\n560: - Need multi-GPU training clusters\n561: - Must use mixed precision (BF16/FP8)\n562: - Quantization essential for any deployment scenario\n563: - Consider MoE architectures for scalability\n564: \n565: ---\n566: \n567: ## Summary: Key Takeaways\n568: \n569: ### Pre-training vs Fine-tuning:\n570: \n571: 1. **Pre-training** builds general representations from massive data; computationally expensive but creates foundation models capable of diverse tasks.\n572: \n573: 2. **Fine-tuning** adapts pre-trained models to specific tasks/domains with much less data and compute; the standard approach for production applications.\n574: \n575: 3. The **pre-training/fine-tuning paradigm** is dominant because it maximizes efficiency: use pre-training once, then fine-tune repeatedly on different tasks.\n576: \n577: ### Inference Optimization:\n578: \n579: 1. **Quantization** (INT8/INT4) reduces memory by 75-90% with minimal accuracy loss; essential for deployment.\n580: \n581: 2. **Pruning** removes unnecessary parameters but requires sparsity-aware hardware or post-pruning fine-tuning.\n582: \n583: 3. **Knowledge Distillation** creates smaller, faster models that mimic larger teacher models' behavior.\n584: \n585: 4. **Attention optimization** (FlashAttention, sparse attention) enables longer context windows and faster inference.\n586: \n587: ### Best Practice Summary:\n588: \n589: - Start with a pre-trained foundation model when possible\n590: - Use fine-tuning for task adaptation; use PEFT if compute-constrained\n591: - Apply quantization before deployment for efficiency\n592: - Implement caching strategies for multi-turn interactions\n593: - Monitor both accuracy and latency during optimization\n594: \n595: ---\n596: \n597: ## References\n598: \n599: 1. Medium (Jan 2026): \"82. Pre-training vs Fine-tuning: The Two-Phase Training Paradigm\" - Explains why the paradigm is dominant due to efficiency, effectiveness, and data requirements.\n600: \n601: 2. Hugging Face Documentation: \"Fine-Tuning\" - Clarifies that fine-tuning uses pre-trained weights (not random initialization) and requires far less compute, data, and time than training from scratch.\n602: \n603: 3. arXiv (Mar 2025): Research on early stopping in pre-training shows it's not detrimental to downstream fine-tuning performance when budget is constrained.\n604: \n605: 4. Lightly AI: Analysis showing pre-training uses large diverse datasets for foundational capabilities while fine-tuning enhances models with labeled task-specific data.\n610: \n611: ---\n612: \n613: *Document created as part of \"Research on Neural Networks and Transformers - Comprehensive Study Documentation\" project.*\n614: *Last updated: 2026-06-21*\n\n(End of file - total 614 lines)\n</content>","metadata":{"preview":"# Transformers in Practice: Pre-training vs Fine-tuning & Inference Strategies\n\n## Table of Contents\n1. [Introduction to Neural Network Training](#introduction-to-neural-network-training)\n5: 2. [Backpropagation Algorithm](#backpropagation-algorithm)\n6: 3. [Optimizers: SGD and Adam](#optimizers-sgd-and-adam)\n7: 4. [Loss Functions](#loss-functions)\n8: 5. [Training Process Overview](#training-process-overview)\n9: \n10: ---\n11: \n12: ## Introduction to Neural Network Training\n13: \n14: Neural network training is the process of adjusting a model's internal parameters (weights and biases) to minimize prediction error on training data. This is achieved through an iterative optimization process that combines:\n15: \n16: - **Forward Propagation**: Computing predictions from input data\n17: - **Loss Computation**: Measuring how wrong those predictions are\n18: - **Backpropagation**: Calculating gradients of the loss with respect to parameters\n19: - **Optimization**: Updating parameters to reduce future error\n20: \n21: The core equation for weight updates is:\n22: \n23: ```\n24: w_new = w_old - learning_rate × gradient\n25: ```\n26: \n27: Where `gradient` comes from backpropagation and `learning_rate` controls step size.\n28: \n29: ---\n30: \n31: ## Backpropagation Algorithm\n32: \n33: ### What is Backpropagation?\n34: \n35: Backpropagation (short for \"backward propagation of errors\") is an efficient algorithm for computing gradients in neural networks. It uses the **chain rule** from calculus to propagate error signals backward through the network layers, calculating how much each weight and bias contributed to the final prediction error.\n36: \n37: ### How Backpropagation Works\n38: \n39: #### Step-by-Step Process:\n40: \n41: 1. **Forward Pass**: Input data flows through the network, producing predictions\n42: 2. **Loss Calculation**: Compute loss between predictions and true labels\n43: 3. **Backward Pass**: \n44:    - Start at output layer\n45:    - Apply chain rule to compute gradients layer by layer\n46:    - For each weight w_ij connecting neurons i and j:\n47: \n48: ```\n49: ∂L/∂w_ij = ∂L/∂a_j × ∂a_j/∂z_j × a_i\n50: ```\n51: \n52: Where:\n53: - `L` = loss function value\n54: - `a_j` = activation of neuron j\n55: - `z_j` = weighted input to neuron j (before activation)\n56: - `a_i` = activation of neuron i\n57: \n58: 4. **Parameter Update**: Adjust weights and biases using gradients\n59: \n60: ### Mathematical Foundation\n61: \n62: #### The Chain Rule Application:\n63: \n64: For a network with layers L₁, L₂, ..., Lₙ, the gradient computation follows:\n65: \n66: ```\n67: ∂L/∂w^(l) = ∂a^(l)/∂z^(l) × ∂z^(l)/∂w^(l)\n68:            = δ^(l) × a^(l-1)\n69: ```\n70: \n71: Where `δ^(l)` is the error term at layer l:\n72: \n73: ```\n74: For output layer (supervised learning):\n75:   δ^L = ∂L/∂a^L × σ'(z^L)\n76: \n77: For hidden layers:\n78:   δ^l = ((w^(l+1))^T × δ^(l+1)) ⊙ σ'(z^l)\n79: ```\n80: \n81: Where `⊙` denotes element-wise multiplication.\n82: \n83: ### Activation Function Derivatives\n84: \n85: The backpropagation gradient depends on activation function derivatives:\n86: \n87: | Activation | Formula | Derivative σ'(z) |\n88: |------------|---------|------------------|\n89: | Sigmoid | 1/(1+e⁻ᶻ) | σ(z)(1-σ(z)) |\n90: | Tanh | tanh(z) | 1 - tanh²(z) |\n91: | ReLU | max(0,z) | 1 if z>0, else 0 |\n92: | LeakyReLU | max(αz, z) | 1 or α |\n93: \n94: ### Gradient Flow Example\n95: \n96: Consider a simple network with one hidden layer:\n97: \n98: ```\n99: Input → [Neuron h₁] → Output neuron o\n100:            ↓\n101:          [Neuron h₂]\n102: ```\n103: \n104: Backpropagation computes:\n105: 1. **Output error**: δ^L = (prediction - target) × σ'(z^L)\n106: 2. **Hidden layer errors**: \n107:    - δ¹_h₁ = w_ho_L × δ^L × σ'(z¹_h₁)\n108:    - δ¹_h₂ = w_ho_L × δ^L × σ'(z¹_h₂)\n109: \n110: ### Why Backpropagation is Efficient\n111: \n112: - **Single backward pass** computes gradients for ALL parameters\n113: - **Reuses intermediate derivatives**: Computation graph approach stores activations from forward pass and reuses them\n114: - **O(n²)** complexity for n-layer networks (vs O(n³) naive approaches)\n115: \n116: ---\n117: \n118: ## Optimizers: SGD and Adam\n119: \n120: ### What is an Optimizer?\n121: \n122: An optimizer determines HOW to update network parameters based on computed gradients. Different optimizers use different strategies to navigate the loss landscape toward minima.\n123: \n124: ---\n125: \n126: ### Stochastic Gradient Descent (SGD)\n127: \n128: #### Algorithm:\n129: \n130: ```\n131: For each mini-batch:\n132:   1. Compute gradient g = ∇_w L(w, batch)\n133:   2. Update weights: w ← w - η × g\n134: ```\n135: \n136: Where `η` is the learning rate.\n137: \n138: #### Key Properties:\n139: \n140: | Property | Description |\n141: |----------|-------------|\n142: | **Update Rule** | Direct descent along negative gradient |\n143: | **Learning Rate** | Fixed or scheduled (e.g., decay over epochs) |\n144: | **Convergence** | Can get stuck in local minima, but often generalizes well |\n145: | **Computational Cost** | Low per iteration |\n146: \n147: #### Learning Rate Scheduling:\n148: \n149: Common strategies to improve SGD convergence:\n150: \n151: ```python\n152: # Step decay\n153: learning_rate = initial_lr * 0.1^(epoch // step_size)\n154: \n155: # Exponential decay  \n156: learning_rate = initial_lr / (1 + decay_rate × epoch)\n157: \n158: # Cosine annealing\n159: learning_rate = min_lr + 0.5 × (max_lr - min_lr) × (1 + cos(π × epoch/total_epochs))\n160: ```\n161: \n162: ---\n163: \n164: ### SGD with Momentum\n165: \n166: #### Algorithm:\n167: \n168: ```python\n169: v_t = μ × v_{t-1} + η × g_t          # Velocity update\n170: w ← w - v_t                          # Weight update\n171: ```\n172: \n173: Where `μ` (mu) is the momentum coefficient, typically 0.9.\n174: \n175: #### How Momentum Works:\n176: \n177: - Accumulates gradients in a moving average way\n178: - Helps escape local minima and saddle points\n179: - Reduces oscillations in parameter updates\n180: - Analogy: Like a ball rolling down a hill with inertia\n181: \n182: ```\n183:         ^\n184:        / \\     (oscillating gradient)\n185:       /   \\\n186: -----/-----\\----→  (smoothed velocity)\n187:     /\n188:    ↓  (momentum carries you forward)\n189: ```\n190: \n191: #### Momentum Values:\n192: \n193: | μ Value | Behavior |\n194: |---------|----------|\n195: | 0.9     | Standard momentum, good balance |\n196: | 0.95+   | High momentum, faster convergence but may overshoot |\n197: | 0.85-0.9| Conservative, more stable training |\n198: \n199: ---\n200: \n201: ### Adam Optimizer (Adaptive Moment Estimation)\n202: \n203: #### Algorithm:\n204: \n205: Adam combines ideas from Momentum and RMSProp with adaptive learning rates per parameter.\n206: \n207: ```python\n208: # First moment estimate (mean of gradients)\n209: m_t = β₁ × m_{t-1} + (1 - β₁) × g_t\n210: \n211: # Second moment estimate (uncentered variance)\n212: v_t = β₂ × v_{t-1} + (1 - β₂) × g²_t\n213: \n214: # Bias correction for initialization\n215: m̂_t = m_t / (1 - β₁^t)\n216: v̂_t = v_t / (1 - β₂^t)\n217: \n218: # Parameter update\n219: w ← w - η × m̂_t / (√(v̂_t) + ε)\n220: ```\n221: \n222: #### Default Hyperparameters:\n223: \n224: | Parameter | Typical Value | Purpose |\n225: |-----------|---------------|---------|\n226: | β₁        | 0.9           | First moment decay rate |\n227: | β₂        | 0.999         | Second moment decay rate |\n228: | ε         | 1e-8          | Numerical stability |\n229: \n230: #### Why Adam is Popular:\n231: \n232: 1. **Adaptive Learning Rates**: Each parameter gets its own learning rate based on gradient history\n233: 2. **Momentum Built-in**: First moment estimation provides momentum-like behavior\n234: 3. **Fast Convergence**: Often requires less tuning than SGD\n235: 4. **Robustness**: Works well across many problems with minimal hyperparameter search\n236: \n237: ---\n238: \n239: ### Optimizer Comparison Table\n240: \n241: | Feature | SGD | SGD+Momentum | RMSProp | Adam |\n242: |---------|-----|--------------|--------|------|\n243: | **Learning Rate** | Global (shared) | Global | Per-parameter adaptive | Per-parameter adaptive |\n244: | **Adaptation Speed** | Slow | Medium | Fast | Very fast |\n245: | **Memory Required** | Low | Medium | Medium | High (stores 2 moments per param) |\n246: | **Hyperparameter Tuning** | High effort | Medium | Medium | Low |\n247: | **Generalization** | Often best | Good | Good | Good, sometimes overfits |\n248: | **Best For** | Large batch, fine-tuning | Standard training | Non-stationary objectives | Quick prototyping, most tasks |\n249: \n250: #### When to Use Each:\n251: \n252: - **SGD with Momentum**: Production models where generalization is critical\n253: - **Adam**: Research/prototyping, when time for tuning SGD is limited\n254: - **RMSProp**: Problems with sparse gradients or non-stationary objectives\n255: - **AMSGrad**: Variant of Adam that avoids overfitting issues in some cases\n256: \n257: ---\n258: \n259: ### Advanced Optimizer Variants\n260: \n261: #### AdamW (Weight Decay Decoupled)\n262: \n263: Separates weight decay from optimization:\n264: \n265: ```python\n266: w ← w - η × m̂_t / (√(v̂_t) + ε) - λ × w  # Weight decay applied directly\n267: ```\n268: \n269: Better than L2 regularization with Adam for many tasks.\n270: \n271: #### Lion Optimizer\n272: \n273: Newer optimizer gaining popularity, simpler than Adam:\n274: \n275: ```python\n276: m_t = μ × m_{t-1} + η × sign(g_t) × g_t\n277: v_t = β₂ × v_{t-1} + (1 - β₂) × |g_t|²\n278: w ← w - η × m̂_t / (√(v̂_t) + ε)\n279: ```\n280: \n281: ---\n282: \n283: ## Loss Functions\n284: \n285: ### What is a Loss Function?\n286: \n287: A loss function (also called cost or objective function) measures the difference between predicted and actual values. The goal of training is to minimize this loss.\n288: \n289: Formally: `L(y, ŷ)` where:\n290: - `y` = true label\n291: - `ŷ` = model prediction\n292: \n293: ---\n294: \n295: ### Mean Squared Error (MSE)\n296: \n297: #### Use Case: Regression problems with continuous targets\n298: \n299: ```python\n300: MSE(y, ŷ) = 1/n × Σ(y_i - ŷ_i)²\n301: ```\n302: \n303: #### Properties:\n304: \n305: | Property | Description |\n306: |----------|-------------|\n307: | **Differentiable** | Yes (smooth gradient everywhere except at exact match) |\n308: | **Penalty Type** | Squared error penalizes large errors more heavily |\n309: | **Gradient** | ∂L/∂ŷ = 2(n-1)(y - ŷ)/n² |\n310: \n311: #### Example: Predicting house prices\n312: \n313: ```python\n314: # True price: $500k, Predicted: $480k\n315: MSE = (500 - 480)² / n = 400/n\n316: \n317: # If prediction was $300k instead:\n318: MSE = (500 - 300)² / n = 40,000/n  # Much worse!\n319: ```\n320: \n321: ---\n322: \n323: ### Binary Cross-Entropy\n324: \n325: #### Use Case: Binary classification (2 classes)\n326: \n327: ```python\n328: BCE(y, p) = -[y × log(p) + (1-y) × log(1-p)]\n329: ```\n330: \n331: Where:\n332: - `y ∈ {0, 1}` is the true label\n333: - `p` is predicted probability of class 1\n334: \n335: #### Properties:\n336: \n337: | Property | Description |\n338: |----------|-------------|\n339: | **Range** | (0, ∞) - always positive |\n340: | **Gradient at p=0.5** | Maximum gradient when prediction is uncertain |\n341: | **Numerical Stability** | Use log-sigmoid trick to avoid overflow |\n342: \n343: #### Implementation with Numerical Stability:\n344: \n345: ```python\n346: import torch\n347: \n348: def stable_bce_loss(y_true, y_pred):\n349:     \"\"\"Stable binary cross-entropy implementation\"\"\"\n350:     eps = 1e-8\n351:     # Clip predictions to avoid log(0)\n352:     p_clipped = torch.clamp(y_pred, eps, 1 - eps)\n353:     loss = -(y_true * torch.log(p_clipped) + \n354:               (1 - y_true) * torch.log(1 - p_clipped))\n355:     return loss.mean()\n356: ```\n357: \n358: ---\n359: \n360: ### Categorical Cross-Entropy\n361: \n362: #### Use Case: Multiclass classification (>2 classes, mutually exclusive)\n363: \n364: ```python\n365: CCE(y, ŷ) = -Σ_j y_j × log(ŷ_j)\n366: ```\n367: \n368: Where:\n369: - `y` is one-hot encoded label (e.g., [0, 1, 0] for class 2 of 3)\n370: - `ŷ` is probability distribution over classes\n371: \n372: #### Properties:\n373: \n374: | Property | Description |\n375: |----------|-------------|\n376: | **Input** | One-hot encoded labels or class indices |\n377: | **Output Layer** | Requires softmax activation |\n378: | **Gradient Flow** | Encourages confident predictions for correct class |\n379: \n380: ---\n381: \n382: ### Hinge Loss (for SVMs)\n383: \n384: #### Use Case: Margin-based classification, particularly with Support Vector Machines\n385: \n386: ```python\n387: Hinge(y, ŷ) = max(0, 1 - y × ŷ)\n388: ```\n389: \n390: Where `y ∈ {-1, +1}` and `ŷ` is the predicted score (not probability).\n391: \n392: #### Properties:\n393: \n394: | Property | Description |\n395: |----------|-------------|\n396: | **Non-differentiable at** | y × ŷ = 1 |\n397: | **SVM Connection** | Foundation of Support Vector Machines |\n398: | **Margin Maximization** | Encourages large margins between classes |\n399: \n400: ---\n401: \n402: ### Loss Function Comparison Table\n403: \n404: | Loss Type | Problem Type | Gradient Behavior | Best For |\n405: |-----------|--------------|-------------------|----------|\n406: | **MSE** | Regression | Smooth everywhere | Continuous targets, Gaussian noise |\n407: | **MAE (L1)** | Regression | Constant gradient magnitude | Robust to outliers |\n408: | **BCE** | Binary Classification | High when uncertain | 2-class problems with sigmoid output |\n409: | **CCE** | Multiclass Classification | Encourages confident predictions | Image classification, NLP token prediction |\n410: | **Hinge** | Margin-based Classification | Non-smooth at boundary | SVM-style models |\n411: | **KL Divergence** | Distribution matching | Smooth, information-theoretic | Generative models, VAEs |\n412: \n413: ---\n414: \n415: ### Advanced Loss Functions\n416: \n417: #### Focal Loss (for Class Imbalance)\n418: \n419: Addresses the problem where easy examples dominate training:\n420: \n421: ```python\n422: Focal(y, p, γ=2) = -α × (1-p)^γ × log(p) if y=1 else -(p)^γ × log(1-p)\n423: ```\n424: \n425: Where `γ` down-weights easy examples.\n426: \n427: #### Label Smoothing\n428: \n429: Prevents model from becoming over-confident:\n430: \n431: ```python\n432: Loss(y, ŷ) = -Σ ((1-ε) × y + ε/K) × log(ŷ)\n433: ```\n434: \n435: Where `K` is number of classes and `ε` is smoothing factor (e.g., 0.1).\n436: \n437: ---\n438: \n439: ## Training Process Overview\n440: \n441: ### Complete Training Pipeline\n442: \n443: ```python\n444: import torch\n445: import torch.nn as nn\n446: from torch.utils.data import DataLoader\n447: \n448: # Define model, loss function, and optimizer\n449: model = YourModel()\n450: criterion = nn.CrossEntropyLoss()  # or MSE, BCE, etc.\n451: optimizer = torch.optim.Adam(model.parameters(), lr=0.001)\n452: \n453: # Training loop\n454: for epoch in range(num_epochs):\n455:     for batch_x, batch_y in train_loader:\n456:         # Forward pass\n457:         optimizer.zero_grad()           # Clear previous gradients\n458:         predictions = model(batch_x)    # Compute outputs\n459:         loss = criterion(predictions, batch_y)  # Compute loss\n460:         \n461:         # Backward pass (backpropagation)\n462:         loss.backward()                 # Compute gradients via .backward()\n463:         \n464:         # Parameter update\n465:         optimizer.step()                # Apply gradient updates\n466:         \n467:         if epoch % 10 == 0:\n468:             print(f\"Epoch {epoch}, Loss: {loss.item():.4f}\")\n469: ```\n470: \n471: ### Key Training Concepts\n472: \n473: #### Learning Rate Schedule Examples:\n474: \n475: ```python\n476: # 1. StepLR - decrease learning rate every step_size epochs\n477: scheduler = torch.optim.lr_scheduler.StepLR(optimizer, \n478:                                             step_size=30, gamma=0.1)\n479: \n480: # 2. ReduceLROnPlateau - decrease when validation loss stops improving\n481: scheduler = torch.optim.lr_scheduler.ReduceLROnPlateau(\n482:     optimizer, mode='min', factor=0.5, patience=5)\n483: \n484: # 3. CosineAnnealingLR - smooth cosine decay\n485: scheduler = torch.optim.lr_scheduler.CosineAnnealingLR(\n486:     optimizer, T_max=num_epochs)\n487: ```\n488: \n489: #### Weight Decay (Regularization):\n490: \n491: Prevents overfitting by penalizing large weights:\n492: \n493: ```python\n494: optimizer = torch.optim.Adam(model.parameters(), \n495:                              lr=0.001, weight_decay=1e-5)\n496: ```\n497: \n498: ---\n499: \n500: ## Quick Reference: Choosing Components\n501: \n502: ### When to Use What:\n503: \n504: #### Optimizer Selection:\n505: \n506: | Scenario | Recommended | Why |\n507: |----------|-------------|-----|\n508: | Research/prototyping | Adam | Fast convergence, less tuning needed |\n509: | Production/fine-tuning | SGD+Momentum | Often better generalization |\n510: | Sparse gradients | RMSProp/Adam | Adaptive learning rates help |\n511: | Very large datasets | AdamW | Decoupled weight decay improves generalization |\n512: \n513: #### Loss Function Selection:\n514: \n515: | Problem Type | Recommended Loss | Output Activation |\n516: |--------------|------------------|-------------------|\n517: | Continuous target (regression) | MSE or MAE | Linear (no activation) |\n518: | 2-class classification | Binary Cross-Entropy | Sigmoid |\n519: | Multi-class (>2) classification | Categorical Cross-Entropy | Softmax |\n520: | Ranking problems | Hinge Loss / Pairwise BCE | No specific activation needed |\n521: \n522: ---\n523: \n524: ## Summary Checklist for Neural Network Training\n525: \n526: ### Before Training:\n527: \n528: - [ ] Select appropriate loss function for your problem type\n529: - [ ] Choose optimizer (Adam for speed, SGD+Momentum for generalization)\n530: - [ ] Set initial learning rate (start with 1e-3 to 1e-4)\n531: - [ ] Consider weight decay if overfitting is a concern\n532: \n533: ### During Training:\n534: \n535: - [ ] Monitor training loss (should decrease monotonically)\n536: - [ ] Track validation metrics (to detect overfitting)\n537: - [ ] Use learning rate scheduling for better convergence\n538: - [ ] Apply data augmentation when possible\n539: \n540: ### After Training:\n541: \n542: - [ ] Evaluate on held-out test set\n543: - [ ] Check if model needs fine-tuning with different hyperparameters\n544: - [ ] Consider ensemble methods if variance is high\n545: \n546: ---\n547: \n548: ## Further Reading\n549: \n550: ### Key Papers:\n551: \n552: 1. **Backpropagation**: Rumelhart, Hinton & Williams (1986) - \"Learning Representations by Back-propagating Errors\"\n553: 2. **Adam Optimizer**: Kingma & Ba (2014) - \"Adam: A Method for Stochastic Optimization\"\n554: 3. **SGD with Momentum**: Polyak & Böttinger (1992)\n555: \n556: ### Recommended Resources:\n557: \n558: - [Gradient Descent and Backpropagation Guide](https://developer.nvidia.com/blog/a-data-scientists-guide-to-gradient-descent-and-backpropagation-algorithms/)\n559: - [Optimizers Comparison Studies](https://apxml.com/courses/deep-learning-regularization-optimization/chapter-6-adaptive-optimizers/choosing-optimizers-guidelines)\n560: \n561: ---\n562: \n563: *Document generated for Neural Networks and Transformers comprehensive study documentation.*\n\n(End of file - This is achieved through an iterative optimization process that combines:\n\n- **Forward Propagation**: Computing predictions from input data\n- **Loss Computation**: Measuring how wrong those predictions are\n- **Backpropagation**: Calculating gradients of the loss with respect to parameters\n- **Optimization**: Updating parameters to reduce future error\n","truncated":false,"loaded":[]},"title":"src/experiment/tmp/judge-5/submission-D/neural_network_training_comprehensive.md","time":{"start":1782016256342,"end":1782016256380}},"metadata":{"openai":{"itemId":"fc_0db6267649e2b1a2016a3769017b788191a6666a9ff688c929"}},"id":"prt_ee8722944001yxYFxImmC0CWO7","sessionID":"ses_1178ef465ffe7X2fG76Cr9xvGX","messageID":"msg_ee871f7700010MOlS0x9fwGkN5"}}
